## user

You are helping build a model library of top-tier LSAT Logical Reasoning explanations for LSAT U (Joshua's LSAT prep platform). The library will be used to write ~540 missing explanations in the same voice and quality. Your job: read candidate explanations for your question types and pick the best 10 to 20 per type as models, and say precisely what makes them model-worthy.

QUESTION TYPES FOR YOU: Principle-Strengthen (justify), and Principle-Conform (apply / illustrate / conform)

INPUT (read in full, every candidate; do not skim):

The heuristic score is only a pre-filter; your judgment decides. Type came from the stem; skip and note any candidate that is clearly a different type.
- Background you should read first (short): C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os\corpus\lr-explanations\golden-set-observations.md sections 3, 4 and 6, and C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os\corpus\lr-explanations\golden-set.md (note if a candidate is already golden).

RUBRIC (0/1/2 each, total /12): P Prediction ("If [evidence facts], then [conclusion judgment]" prephrased, or the slogan for implicit principles, or the trigger that must be established when the principle is in the stimulus); X Precision; E Eliminations (every wrong answer diagrammed or tested: does the trigger apply? does the outcome match the conclusion? judgment on the wrong side / wrong party / wrong judgment; no verdict-only notes; the most popular wrong answer by student % gets the most attention and why it tempts); T Transfer; C Clarity; L Learner value.
DISQUALIFY or flag: a choice note that misquotes or describes a different choice than printed; outside facts used to justify the key; mocking the question; heavy em-dash formulaic register; correct note arguing for the wrong key; unreadable transcription damage (flattened diagrams). WHAT TO PICK: 10 to 20 per type (at least 10 if deserved). Spread across difficulty and sub-patterns: principles in answers, principle in stimulus (apply it / justify its application), implicit principles, weighing tradeoffs, two principles. Prefer variety.

## 3. What separates strong from loose (the empirical comparison)

I picked ten explanations that were clearly strong and ten that were complete but loose, read all twenty side by side, then measured a few things.

- **Strong (10):** PT139-S4-Q18 Flaw, PT142-S4-Q22 Flaw, PT145-S2-Q19 Paradox, PT154-S1-Q18 NA, PT153-S2-Q13 Weaken, PT146-S2-Q13 Strengthen, PT147-S1-Q17 Evaluate, PT152-S2-Q21 Principle-Conform, PT150-S3-Q15 Agree/Disagree, PT144-S3-Q17 Role.
- **Loose but complete (10), with the failure each shows:** PT127-S2-Q3 Weaken (two wrong answers get one line each: "This supports the argument's explanation"); PT126-S4-Q20 Weaken (generic summary notes, a Goal that restates the task); PT124-S3-Q10 Role (short, and "Haha. This answer is unthinkably weird"); PT151-S2-Q18 Strengthen (117 words total; the 29% trap gets one sentence); PT146-S2-Q8 Flaw (every wrong answer is "The argument never discusses X", correct note is one line); PT142-S4-Q5 Principle (mechanical, no reason why the trap tempts); PT155-S2-Q10 Paradox (fine idea, thin notes); PT158-S3-Q2 Strengthen (long, but misquotes a choice); PT158-S2-Q16 Paradox (long, but brings in outside facts); PT148-S1-Q20 Method (sound analysis, but calls the correct answer "so, so dumb").

Measured averages (strong vs loose):

| Measure | Strong | Loose |
|---|---|---|
| Total words | 773 | 394 |
| Words in the Evaluate step | 169 | 77 |
| Words in the Goal step | 66 | 43 |
| Goal names 3+ content words from the stimulus | 0.9 | 0.5 |
| Avg words per wrong-answer note | 87 | 45 |
| Wrong-answer notes under 20 words (verdict only) | 0.0 per question | 1.1 per question |
| Wrong-answer notes that run an explicit test (negate it, does it weaken, does it trigger the rule, why should we believe it) | 1.4 of 4 | 0.2 of 4 |
| Words in the correct-answer note | 143 | 60 |
| Examples, analogies or worked numbers | 2.0 | 0.5 |
| Stated generalizations ("whenever", "on X questions", "usually") | 1.6 | 0.3 |

What actually separates them, in order of how reliably it predicted my judgment:

1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument.** Strong ones do real work before the choices: they build the objection ("Given that X, how could we argue not-Y?"), do the arithmetic (25% of $400 vs 13% of $1,000), or set the two populations side by side. Loose ones restate the stimulus and move on.
2. **The Goal names the answer, not the task.** Strong: "look for an answer saying the fish in the leafless trees are exposed to predators that hunt by sight." Loose: "Find the flaw that mistakes a response to danger for a cause", "Identify an answer choice that supports fairness." Strong Goals often give two acceptable shapes (a linking answer or a defender).
3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason tied to this question, and usually a test.** The loose group's signature is the verdict-only note: "This is irrelevant.", "That is unnecessary.", "The argument never discusses profits." Strong notes say what the answer claims, what was needed, and where they diverge, often by running the family's test on it (negate it; would it weaken; is the trigger established; does the conclusion match the rule's right side).
4. **The popular trap gets the most attention.** Strong explanations spend their longest wrong-answer note on the choice that drew 15-30% of students and say why it tempts. Loose ones treat it like every other wrong answer.
5. **The correct-answer note explains the fit, and often generalizes it.** Strong: "Another correct answer performing the same function could have said..." (PT146-S2-Q13); the karaoke/Jenga reason why A → C still works when you predicted A and B → C (PT139-S4-Q21). Loose: "This is the flaw."
6. **Fidelity.** Two loose items are long. Length did not save them: a misquoted answer choice, imported outside facts, or the author disowning the question destroys model value regardless of word count.

What did not separate them: word count by itself (PT152-S4-Q19 is 479 words and strong; PT158-S2-Q16 is 716 and loose), the choice-tag vocabulary, and the presence of jokes (several strong ones joke; it only hurts when the joke is at the question's or the answer's expense).

## 4. Rubric used for the shortlist

Gate (hard requirement): full Argument breakdown and all five choice notes. Every shortlisted candidate passed.

Scored 0 / 1 / 2:

| Criterion | 2 | 1 | 0 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **P** Prediction | Goal names the content or shape of the right answer before the choices (may give two acceptable shapes) | Goal names the task or admits it cannot predict | No prediction |
| **X** Precision | Names the exact gap, flaw or relationship in this argument's words | Uses the right label but stays generic | Label only, or wrong |
| **E** Eliminations | Every wrong answer gets a question-grounded reason; the popular trap gets the most attention | Most do; one or two verdict-only notes | Verdict-only notes |
| **T** Transfer | States the move in a form that works on the next question of this type (the test, the rule, the recurring trap) | Transfer is implicit | None |
| **C** Clarity | Plain, no padding, no pasted repetition | Some padding or pasted notes | Hard to follow |
| **L** Learner value | Teaches what a student needs to understand and repeat the move: why the trap tempts, what to do when stuck, a worked example or analogy, a second correct answer that would also work | Justifies the key well but does not teach the move | Only justifies the key |

Plus a **fidelity flag** (not scored; disqualifying if serious): `slip` factual or typo error, `tone` the author mocks the question or answer, `register` explicit-Standard PT157/158 prose, `transcription` damaged text.

Learner value is the criterion that most often separated a 10 from a 12. Many explanations justify the key correctly (1) but only the best ones also show the student the move they will reuse (2).

## 5. Step structure per family (DB, most common sequences)

Labels normalized (parenthetical indicator words dropped: "Conclusion (therefore)" counts as Conclusion).

- **Flaw (524):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (67%); Evidence > Conclusion > Evaluate (9%); with an Intermediate Conclusion step (5%). Evaluate usually names the famous flaw or builds the objection; Goal often gives both the formal and the "fails to consider" phrasing.
- **Must Be True / Most Supported (462):** Statements > Evaluate > Goal (45%); Statements only (16%). Statements is often a diagram. Evaluate scans for conditional, causal, math and pivot wording and names the inference to make.
- **Necessary Assumption (352):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (66%). Goal typically offers a linking answer and a defender answer, and says "negate it".
- **Weaken (335) / Strengthen (277):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (59% / 55%). Evaluate uses "Given that ... how could we argue that ..." or Curious Fact / Alternate Explanation / Plausibility.
- **Paradox (213):** "Apparent Paradox > Evaluate" (22%, older tests) or "Given that ... > How can it be that / Why is it that ... > Evaluate > Goal" (about 30% combined, often with Evaluate / Goal merged).
- **Sufficient Assumption (193):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (53%); Evidence > Conclusion > Evaluate (21%). Goal usually diagrams the missing link as the logic path.
- **Principle-Strengthen (181):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (48%). Goal is "If evidence, then conclusion" in general terms.
- **Principle-Conform (99):** mixed. Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (24%) when the principle is in the answers; Principle > Evaluate > Goal (9%) or Principle > Goal (7%) when it is in the stimulus.
- **Main Conclusion (144) / Role (126):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (42% / 53%), with an Intermediate Conclusion step in about 8%. Evaluate almost always opens with the reminder that the conclusion usually comes early (first sentence or after a pivot).
- **Parallel (100) / Parallel Flaw (123):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (49% / 67%), Evidence first in about 19-35%. Goal is an algebraic recipe (P1, P2, C) or a plain-language template.
- **Agree/Disagree (119):** speaker-named steps. "Speaker's Claims > Speaker's Responses > Goal", often a numbered claim list with "Does [speaker 2] disagree?" per claim.
- **Method (91):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (37%) for one speaker; "X's Conclusion > X's Evidence > Y's Response > Evaluate > Goal" for two speakers.
- **Evaluate (28):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (75%).

## 6. Recurring phrasing conventions

- **Frames:** "Given that ... how could we (still) argue that ..." (Weaken, Strengthen, NA, Flaw); "Curious Fact ... Author's Explanation ... Alternate Explanation / Plausibility" (any causal argument); "Given that ... How can it be that ..." (Paradox); "If Evidence, then Conclusion" (Principle, SA).
- **Tests in choice notes:** negate it and ask if it weakens (NA); "fails to consider X" means ask whether X weakens, "presumes X" means ask whether X is necessary (Flaw); "argues X by Y: X must match the conclusion and Y the evidence" (Method, Flaw); a rule can only prove its right-hand side (Principle, SA); "can we support the Agree position and the Disagree position?" (Agree/Disagree); "Why should we believe this?" (Role, Main Conclusion).
- **Recurring trap names:** Too Strong (the most common NA and MBT tag), Out of Scope, No Impact, Unclear Impact, No Distinction (Paradox), Deepens Paradox, Wrong Flaw / Not an Objection (Flaw), Bad Conclusion / Evidence / Trigger Match (Principle, Parallel), Premise / Last-Claim Trap / Opposing Position (Main Conclusion), Unsupported Agree/Disagree Position (Agree/Disagree), Fake Opposite / Illegal Negation (NA), Reversal (SA). Tags come from the local markdown; the dump does not include them.
- **Rules stated as rules:** "Most X can only be concluded from a Most X premise"; "some / sometimes / can is almost always wrong on Strengthen, Weaken, Paradox, SA"; "the second speaker almost never denies the first speaker's evidence"; "about 85-90% of Main Conclusion questions put the conclusion in the first sentence or after a pivot"; "on EXCEPT, three of the working answers usually work one way and one works another".
- **Voice habits that should not be copied:** mocking the question or answer ("hot garbage", "so, so dumb", "unthinkably weird", "dumpster fire"), and the PT157/158 em-dash register.

5	STIMULUS: Literary critic: There is little of social significance in contemporary novels, for readers cannot enter the internal world of the novelist’s mind unless they experience that world from the moral perspective of the novel’s characters. But in contemporary novels, the transgressions committed by some characters against others are sensationalistic spectacles whose only purpose is to make readers wonder what will happen next, rather than events whose purpose is to be seen as the injustices they are.
6	STEM: Which one of the following principles, if valid, would most help to justify the literary critic’s argument?
7	(A) [2% | Out of Scope: should] An artist who wants to engage the moral sensibilities of his or her audience should not assume that forms of artistic expression that previously served this purpose continue to do so.
8	(B) [21% | Out of Scope: should] A novelist who wants to make a reader empathize with a victim of injustice should avoid sensationalistic spectacles whose only purpose is to make readers wonder what will happen next.
9	(C) [17% | Weaker Trigger Match] A work of art is socially important only if it engages the moral sensibilities of its audience.
10	(D) [15% | Reversed Logic] If a novel allows a reader to understand injustice from the point of view of its victims, it will be socially significant.
11	(E) [45% | Correct] Novels have social significance only to the extent that they allow readers to enter the internal world of the novelist’s mind.
12	--- BREAKDOWN
13	## Conclusion
14	There is little of social significance in contemporary novels.
15	## Evidence
16	```
17	can't experience world from moral perspective of novel's characters
18	  → can't enter internal world of the novelist's mind
19	```
20	In contemporary novels, the (moral) transgressions are just spectacles, not events whose purpose is to be seen as the injustices they are.
21	## Evaluate
22	Good Lord, argument, I want to award you some Reddit badge for "Least readable". This is not fun to digest. I'm relying a lot on stopping as I read and saying to myself where this is going / what I'll need to hear. If I said, "There is nothing fun about ferris wheels, because you cannot confuse the body's senses unless you go fast", there are two implicit assumptions there: 1. Ferris wheels don't go fast 2. If you don't confuse the body's senses, it's not fun. Try it on the original first sentence: "There is little of social significance in contemporary novels, because you can't enter internal world of writer's mind unless you experience that world from the moral perspective of the novel's characters". What are the two assumptions? 1. In contemporary novels, you don't experience the world from the moral perspective of the novel's characters. 2. If you don't enter the internal world of the writer's mind, then there is little social significance. The 2nd sentence (yes, shockingly, this is only two sentences) is trying to establish one of these negatives. It's saying "in contemporary novels, X is this, rather than that." We would probably try to match this with #1 above. They're trying to establish that you don't experience the world from the moral perspective of the novel's characters. They say that the transgressions committed (immoral actions) are not events whose purpose is to be seen as the injustices they are.
23	## Goal
24	This would make me think we're headed towards, "if moral transgressions aren't presented as events whose purpose is to be seen as the injustices they are, then you won't be able to experience the world of the novel from the moral perspective of the novel's characters." But given how hard this feels, I'd be willing to consider other missing links, and I'm planning to mainly get rid of wrong answers by finding stuff that sounds Too Strong or Out of Scope.
25	--- CHOICE NOTES
26	(A) Phew, a quick bailout with "should". The author doesn't use any normative language, so we're not allowed to be assigning her any normative assumptions.
27	
28	She is just describing contemporary novels as lacking in social significance. That doesn't necessarily mean that the author thinks that contemporary novels should change.
29	
30	I could describe how Starburst candy is devoid of nutritional value but not be assuming "Starburst should put more protein and vitamins in their candy".
31	(B) Awesome, another answer with "should". The author doesn't use any normative language, so we're not allowed to be assigning her any normative assumptions.
32	
33	She is just describing contemporary novels as lacking in social significance. She doesn't need to make any assumptions about what "should" be the case.
34	(C) This is fairly close. It's saying
35	```
36	doesn't engage moral sensibilities of audience → not socially important
37	```
38	The conclusion is essentially saying
39	```
40	can't experience world from moral perspective of novel's characters → little of social significance
41	```
42	The outcomes are pretty interchangeable. If we know something is not socially important, then we can definitely support the idea that there's "little of social significance".
43	
44	But the trigger doesn't match. As an audience member, having your moral sensibilities engaged is very different from experiencing the world from the moral perspective of a character.
45	
46	Perhaps a character is having an affair, but they feel justified in doing so because they blame their spouse for a loveless marriage. They don't consider their affair wrong.
47	
48	An audience member experiencing the world from the moral perspective of that character would agree there's nothing wrong with the affair. But a different audience member might be repulsed by the affair and think that the character is totally wrong for betraying their spouse. That audience member still had their moral sensibilities engaged, but didn't experience the world from the moral perspective of the character.
49	(D) This is the ol' Illegal Lightswitch move, where they flip both ideas.

54	```

61	The word "for" in the first sentence (of FABS fame ... for, after all, because, since) shows that the first clause is a conclusion being supported by what comes after "for".
62	
63	The author is indicating that whether or not a novel has much social significance is related to whether or not readers can enter the internal world of the novelist's mind.
64

68	and thus
69	you don't experience the novel's world from the moral perspective of the novel's characters
70	and thus
71	you can't cant enter the internal world of the novelist's mind,

83	(B) [4% | Bad Trigger Match] The leader of a country should resign if doing so is likely to improve that country’s international reputation for political stability.
84	(C) [1% | Bad Trigger Match] If a president is involved in a scandal that is more serious than scandals that have forced previous leaders to resign, then that president should resign.
85	(D) [5% | Bad Trigger Match / Bad Conlusion Match] If it can be conclusively proven that an officeholder rigged an election, then that officeholder should be removed from office.

Given President Z's concern with what effects her resignation could have on how the world sees this country, how can the activist argue that President Z should still resign? Can he say, "So what if the world sees us as politically unstable. We are. That's the truth. It's okay for the world to see the truth"? He could probably also say, "The world already sees us as politically unstable, given that there's a widespread belief that you rigged the election. So we shouldn't refrain from doing what's right out of concern that we'll start to look politically unhinged. We've already achieved that status. We could probably upgrade the world's impression of our political system if they saw that you, a seemingly illegitimate president, were no longer at the helm."
98	## Goal
99	Look for something that addresses Person 2's objection, from the point of view of Person 1. In other words, if we're someone who thinks that President Z should resign, then how can we respond to her notion that "resigning would lead to the world having an unacceptably low opinion of our political system"?

130	(D) Neither half of this rule matches up. The conclusion is that Zagel "should resign", not that Zagel "should be removed from office".
131	That's enough to move on.
132	We also couldn't establish the trigger, because we can't say that it's been "conclusively proven" that Zagel rigged the election.

135	Meanwhile, Comparative answers are more like strengtheners than they are like provers. They won't firmly settle the conclusion, but they can tilt us more in that direction.

451	STIMULUS: Curator: Critics have rightly claimed that removing the centuries-old grime from the frescoes of Michelangelo will expose them to acids formed by the combination of water vapor in human breath with pollutants in the air. 455	(C) [88% | Correct] It is acceptable to risk future damage to an artwork if the purpose is to enable it to be appreciated in its original form.
456	(D) [1% | Bad Conclusion / Evidence Match] It is right to spend large amounts of money on the restoration of an old artwork if this restoration makes the artwork accessible to large numbers of people.
457	(E) [8% | Bad Conclusion Match] A picture that has become encrusted with grime over a long period can no longer be regarded as the same work of art as that painted by the artist.

461	## Evidence (for)
462	The frescoes in their present condition cannot be seen as they appeared when painted by Michelangelo.
463	## Evaluate
464	We start by anticipating answers on Strengthen + Principle by simply saying some version of "If Premise, then Conclusion": If frescoes can't be seen we should as they appeared when → do / continue the painted by Michelangelo restoration But since the answers on Principle questions are more generic and universal, we might try to be a little less specific in our trigger: If an artwork presently we should can't be seen as it appeared → try to restore to the artist who made it its orig form Some answers are phrased conditionally, but still reference the Conclusion and the Evidence, so we might see an answer like, "the decision over whether or not to restore a piece of art should be based on whether it can presently be seen as it appeared when it was first created" or like "the most important factor in considering whether to restore a painting is trying to make it look the way it appeared when first painted". However, this argument has a counterpremise as well, a reason we shouldn't do the restoration. When a Strengthen + Principle question mentions an upside as well as a downside, we sometimes refer to it as Weighing Tradeoffs. The upside of doing the restoration is that we remove the grime and get the frescoes back to appearing like they did when painted by Michelangelo. The downside of doing the restoration is that we'll expose them to air and breath that will potentially erode the original paint more quickly. If the author is saying we should not do the restoration, then they have a principle in their minds like, "It's more important that we protect these paintings from exposure to erosive substances than that we make them appear like they originally did".

487	=================== PT145-S2-Q21 | diff 2 | key A | score 20.0 | features: Missing Link / Idea, Principles in Stimulus
488	STIMULUS: Principle: If one does not criticize a form of behavior in oneself or vow to stop it, then one should not criticize that form of behavior in another. Application: If Shimada does not vow to stop being tardy himself, he should not criticize McFeney for tardiness.
489	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, justifies the above application of the principle?
490	(A) [87% | Correct] Both McFeney and Shimada are regularly tardy, but Shimada criticizes McFeney’s tardiness without criticizing his own.
491	(B) [1% | Unrelated to Goal] McFeney is regularly tardy, but Shimada is almost never tardy.
492	(C) [2% | Unrelated to Goal] McFeney often criticizes Shimada for being tardy, but neither Shimada nor McFeney ever vows to cease being tardy.
493	(D) [9% | Opposite of Goal] Shimada criticizes McFeney for regularly being tardy, but also criticizes himself for occasional tardiness.
494	(E) [1% | Unrelated to Goal] Neither McFeney nor Shimada is regularly tardy, but Shimada criticizes McFeney for tardiness nonetheless.
495	--- BREAKDOWN
496	## Principle
497	```
498	not criticizing yourself                          shouldn't criticize
499	for behavior X                    and    →         someone else
500	not vowing to stop X                               for doing X
501	```
502	## Application
503	```
504	Shimada doesn't vow to        →        shouldn't criticize
505	stop being tardy himself               M for tardiness
506	```
507	## Evaluate
508	Principle-Application questions are dreamy, because they are very self-similar. 1. We're given a rule 2. The application is trying to conclude the right side of the rule 3. Something about the left side of the rule still hasn't been clearly established, and that's our whole job -- figuring out what that is. This application is indeed concluding the right side of the rule, shouldn't criticize someone else for [tardiness]. Have we established that "Shimada doesn't criticize himself for being tardy" and that "Shimada does not vow to stop being tardy himself?" Not quite. We only established that Shimada hasn't vowed to stop being tardy himself. Why do we need both? Isn't the rule saying or? It does seem that way at first, but if it were really an or trigger, then saying that Shimada doesn't vow to stop being tardy himself would be sufficient to trigger the rule. If the application were already a correct usage of the rule, then there would be no room for them to ask us a Strengthen question. You can't strengthen something that's already valid. So part of how we'd figure out that the trigger is and, not or, is just by understanding that something has to be missing currently. When you say that something is not X or Y, you're saying ~ (X or Y). This is almost like distributing a -1 in algebra. ~ (X or Y) = ~X and ~Y. Think of a more conversational example: If you are not vaccinated or recovered from covid, then you should wear your mask. When we say "X or Y → Z", we could re-write that as X → Z and Y → Z. When we see, If you are not vaccinated or recovered from covid, then you should wear your mask, if we treated it the same way as the above, we'd get not vaccinated → should wear mask, recovered from covid → should wear mask. That second one doesn't make any sense. If you've had covid and recovered, then you should wear a mask? That's not what we meant. We were saying if you haven't already had and recovered from covid, then you should wear a mask. This trigger, If you are not vaccinated or recovered from covid, is really meaning "If you are not vaccinated or (not) recovered". But the "not" isn't written again there because it's modifying the "vaccinated or recovered". The trigger is saying, "If you're not belonging to the crew of people who are (X or Y), then you should wear your mask." To not belong to that crew means that you have not been vaccinated and have not already recovered from covid. The tl;dr version of that is, when we say "if you're not X or Y", we're saying, "If it is not the case that at least one of these two things is true about you". So we're saying, "If it is the case that neither of these things is true about you".
509	## Goal
510	We have half the trigger (hasn't vowed to stop being tardy), but we need the other half of the trigger (doesn't criticize himself for his tardiness).
511	--- CHOICE NOTES
512	(A) This establishes that tardiness "is a form of behavior in Shimada" as well as "a form of behavior in McFeney", and it establishes that Shimada doesn't criticize his own tardiness. That's what we were missing. Now that we've established that Shimada is tardy, doesn't criticize himself for it , and doesn't vow to stop being tardy, we can apply this principle and justifiably conclude, "Shimada shouldn't criticize tardiness in others".
513	(B) We need to know that Shimada doesn't criticize himself for his tardiness, or else we haven't established the trigger condition of this principle.
514	(C) We need to know that Shimada doesn't criticize himself for his tardiness, or else we haven't established the trigger condition of this principle. This only says that Shimada gets criticized by others for being tardy. We need to know whether Shimada ever criticizes himself for his tardiness.
515	(D) We need to know that Shimada doesn't criticize himself for his tardiness, but this says that he does.
516	(E) We need to know that Shimada doesn't criticize himself for his tardiness, or else we haven't established the trigger condition of this principle.

573	=================== PT137-S3-Q24 | diff 5 | key B | score 19.6 | features: Principles in Answers, Missing Link / Idea
574	STIMULUS: Music critic: How well an underground rock group's recordings sell is no mark of that group's success as an underground group. After all, if a recording sells well, it may be because some of the music on the recording is too trendy to be authentically underground; accordingly, many underground musicians consider it desirable for a recording not to sell well. But weak sales may simply be the result of the group's incompetence.
575	STEM: Which one of the following principles, if valid, most helps to justify the music critic's argument?
576	(A) [9% | Bad Outcome Match Opposite (if anything)] If an underground rock group is successful as an underground group, its recordings will sell neither especially well nor especially poorly.
577	(B) [48% | Correct] An underground rock group is unsuccessful as an underground group if it is incompetent or if any of its music is too trendy to be authentically underground, or both.
578	(C) [18% | Weaker Impact] Whether an underground group's recordings meet criteria that many underground musicians consider desirable is not a mark of that group's success.
579	(D) [16% | Reversal / Negation] An underground rock group is successful as an underground group if the group is competent but its recordings nonetheless do not sell well.
580	(E) [9% | Opposite (if anything)] For an underground rock group, competence and the creation of authentically underground music are not in themselves marks of success.
581	--- BREAKDOWN
582	## Conclusion
583	How well an underground rock group's recordings sell is no mark of that group's success as an underground group.
584	## Evidence (after all)
585	You could have strong sales, but not for a cool reason, rather because your music is too trendy to be authentically undergroun. You could have weak sales, but not for a cool reason, rather because you're just incompetent.
586	## Evaluate
587	This argument can be hard to understand. It helps to start by thinking of how you prove a claim like "X is no mark of Y", for example, "How attractive you are is no mark of how good a person you are". Maybe some people think "beautiful people are good people". But we could show them that beautiful people are sometimes bad. Maybe people think the opposite: "beautiful people are bad people". But we could show them that beautiful people are sometimes good. Ultimately, we're trying to show that beauty doesn't go hand in hand with good or bad. An attractive person might be good or bad, just as an unattractive person might be good or bad. Some people might think that if a band's recording sells really well, then they are a success! No, our author says, because sometimes a band sells well but their music is too trendy to be authentically underground. The author is assuming that they're not a success, if they're music isn't authentic / it's just trendy. Some people might actually think to be a successful underground group, you should be so weird and provocative that you aren't popular, that your recordings don't sell well. But, no, our author says, because you might sell poorly just because you're incompetent. The author is assuming that if you're incompetent, then you're not successful.
588	## Goal
589	Essentially this author is thinking that SUCCESS as an underground group requires being authentic and competent. She thinks that if you're too trendy to be authentic, or if you're incompetent, then you're not really a success. Let's look for an answer that strengthens any of these missing links.

It's easier to trigger those rules, since you only need to establish one fact to trigger them. You can't trigger an AND rule unless you establish two facts.

138	STIMULUS: A gift is not generous unless it is intended to benefit the recipient and is worth more than what is expected or customary in the situation; a gift is selfish if it is given to benefit the giver or is less valuable than is customary.
139	STEM: Which one of the following judgments most closely conforms to the principle above?
140	(A) [18% | Bad Trigger Match] Charles, who hates opera, was given two expensive tickets to the opera. He in turn gave them to his cousin, who loves opera, as a birthday gift. Charles's gift was selfish because he paid nothing for the tickets.
141	(B) [6% | Bad Trigger Match] Emily gives her brother a year's membership in a health club. She thinks that this will allow her brother to get the exercise he needs. However, the gift is selfish because Emily's brother is hurt and offended by it.
142	(C) [5% | Bad Conclusion Match] Amanda gives each of her clients an expensive bottle of wine every year. Amanda's gifts are generous, since they cause the clients to continue giving Amanda business.
143	(D) [62% | Correct] Olga gives her daughter a computer as a graduation gift. Since this is the gift that all children in Olga's family receive for graduation, it is not generous.
144	(E) [9% | Bad Trigger Match] Michael gave his nephew $50 as a birthday gift, more than he had ever given before. Michael's nephew, however, lost the money. Therefore, Michael's gift was not generous because it did not benefit the recipient.
145	--- BREAKDOWN
146	## Principle 1
147	not intended to benefit recipient gift not or → generous not worth more than what is expected or typical in the situation
148	## Principle 2
149	given to benefit giver or → gift is selfish less valuable than customary
150	## Evaluate
151	The first rule is triggered by **unless**, which means the same thing as " **if** it is **not** the case that".
152	When we see, "It is not X unless it's Y", then we could rephrase that as, " **if it is not the case** that it's Y, then it is not X". ~Y → ~X
153	There's a tricky aspect here, though because we're applying the "if-not" to a complex idea. If we're given, "It is not X unless it's Y and Z", then we're saying, "If it's not the case [that it's both Y and Z], then it's not X".
154	Negating (Y and Z) turns it into ~Y or ~Z.
155	That makes sense, since we know that if Y is not true, then it's certainly "not the case that both Y and Z are true". Similarly, as soon as we know Z is not true, then it's not the case that Y and Z are true.
156	Thus, "it is not X unless it's Y and Z" becomes ~Y **or** ~Z → ~X
157	The second conditional is easier to deal with because the word " **if** " indicates the idea that goes on the trigger. So "it is X **if** it is Y or Z" becomes, Y or Z → X
158	## Goal
159	When we're provided with two conditional principles, the correct answer (almost?) never uses both of them. So the first thing we do when we read each answer is check to see what it's conclusion is.
160	If it's concluding "not generous", then we'll use rule 1 and see whether the answer choice establishes the trigger.
161	If it's concluding "selfish", then we'll use rule 2 and see whether the answer choice establishes that rule's trigger.
162	If it's concluding "it is generous" or "it isn't selfish", we'll just throw it out without reading it. These rules have no power to prove something is generous or isn't selfish.
163	--- CHOICE NOTES
164	(A) Since this is concluding "selfish", we'll see whether the argument establishes the trigger for rule 2:
165

259	=================== PT148-S4-Q6 | diff 2 | key A | score 20.4 | features: Analogies, Causal Difference-Maker, Principles in Answers
260	STIMULUS: Engineer: Wide roads free of obstructions have been shown to encourage drivers to take more risks. Likewise, a technical fix to slow or reverse global warming by blocking out a portion of the sun’s rays would encourage more carbon dioxide emissions, which might cause more global warming in the future.
261	STEM: The engineer’s argument can most reasonably be interpreted as invoking which one of the following principles?
262	(A) [84% | Correct] Conditions that create a feeling of security also encourage risk taking.
263	(B) [1% | Too Strong: require / Weak Match: created by humans] Problems created by humans require human-created solutions.
264	(C) [6% | Too Strong: inevitably / Weak Match: technical fixes] Technical fixes are inevitably temporary.
265	(D) [8% | Too Strong: cannot / Weak Match: technical fixes] Technical fixes cannot discourage risk-taking behavior.
266	(E) [1% | Out of Scope: unresolved / worse] The longer a problem goes unresolved, the worse it becomes.
267	--- BREAKDOWN
268	## Conclusion (Implied, Likewise)
269	Blocking out some of the sun to slow or reverse global warming is analogous to making wide roads free of obstructions to reduce accidents.
270	## Evidence
271	With wide, unobstructed roads, drivers feel like they can take more risks, so the expected uptick in driving safety is somewhat offset by this riskier driving.
272	
273	With blocking out some of the sun's rays to cool the earth, people/corporations would feel like they can emit more CO2, so the expected cooling effect is somewhat offset by higher emissions.
274	## Evaluate
275	It's a stretch to call this an *argument*, as the question stem does. And it's a stretch to call this a *Necessary Assumption* question as our tagging does.
276	
277	But when we're given an argument and asked which principle in the answer choices best conforms to that argument, it's similar to being asked, "Which of these answers was the author clearly thinking, in making that reasoning move?", which is the gist of a Necessary Assumption question.
278	
279	There are a handful of Necessary Assumption questions on the last 20 LSATs that are really analogies. Most of them still have identifiable conclusions, but some don't. In either case, we want to focus on thinking about the intended analogy.
280	
281	Analogies are two different sets of specifics, which share some general trait / relationship / truism.
282	
283	The correct answer to these will feel like it's speaking to something that is true in both cases.
284	## Goal
285	We want something like, "Attempts to improve X can backfire". "As you reduce a threat, other people feel more emboldened to fill that vacuum."
286	--- CHOICE NOTES
287	(A) Yes, this is true in both halves of the analogy.
288	
289	Making a road wider and clearing it of obstructions makes drivers feel more security that they're not going to run into other cars or obstacles.
290	
291	Does that encourage risk taking? Yes, wide and unobstructed roads "have been shown to encourage drivers to take more risks".
292	
293	Blocking out part of the sun to reduce the threat of global warming makes polluters feel more secure that their CO2 emissions will not imperil the future habitability of Earth for humans.
294	
295	Does that encourage risk taking? Yes, it "encourages more carbon dioxide emissions (which risks causing more global warming in the future)".
296	
297	Interestingly, this answer definitely requires a common sense notion that "less global warming = more feeling of security".
298	(B) Is this answer true for both halves of the analogy?
299	
300	When we make a road wider and clear it of obstructions, can we say that we're solving a problem created by humans?
301	
302	Hmm. Maybe. The thing motivating us to widen a road would be that there are too many cars for the existing width to feel safe.
303	
304	Is the author stressing that such a thing requires human-created solutions?
305	
306	No, there's nothing like that. First of all, what other solutions are we talking about? Beavers are going to clear the logjam of our congested highway? Robots are going to innovate and implement a road re-design project?
307	
308	Practically all solutions are human-created solutions, unless we're talking about the time scale of evolutionary "solutions" (i.e. adaptations).
309	
310	The paragraph also doesn't say that global warming is a problem created by humans, nor does it stress that humans will be needed to solve global warming.
311	(C) Is this answer true for both halves of the analogy?
312	
313	When we make a road wider and clear it of obstructions, can we say that doing a technical fix?
314	
315	Hmm. Maybe. But we're more renovating / re-designing something than fixing it. It's also not clear that clearing obstructions is a technical fix. Taking a stray mattress off the side of the road? Removing fallen tree branches? Those are technical fixes?
316	
317	Is the author stressing that the wideness and unobstructed-ness is inevitably temporary? Soon, it will be narrower and obstructed again?
318	
319	No, that's awkward to match up.
320	
321	Furthermore, using our Necessary Assumption brain should be trying to be conservative with language, so inevitably should scare us a little, unless we see something in the passage to match that drama.
322	(D) Is this answer true for both halves of the analogy?
323	
324	As we discussed for (C), it feels a bit stretchy to say that the 1st half was dealing with a technical fix.
325	
326	Is the author stressing that the wideness and unobstructed-ness cannot discourage risk-taking behavior? That's a bit of a leap. but if the author is saying that it encourages drivers to take more risks, then I guess it doesn't discourage drivers from taking more risks.
327	
328	But, using our Necessary Assumption brain, which is scared by strong language ... can we feel good saying that this author thinks that zero technical fixes ever have discouraged risk-taking behavior?
329	
330	That seems far too strong.
331	(E) Is this answer true for both halves of the analogy?
332	
333	When we make a road wider and clear it of obstructions, can we say that a problem is going unresolved for longer?
334	
335	No, that would seemingly be the opposite. We're attempting to resolve a problem, by making roads wider and less obstructed.
336	
337	However, we're allowed to invert Volume Dial statements. This answer also means, "the sooner a problem gets resolved, the less worse it becomes".
338	
339	Is the author stressing that "the sooner we widen the roads and clear the obstructions, the less of a problem we'll have with roadway accidents"?
340	
341	No, she's stressing something that moves in the opposite direction. We'll attempt to resolve the problem, but it will prompt a behavior that actually adds back something onto the problem.

344	=================== PT134-S2-Q23 | diff 3 | key D | score 20.3 | features: Principles in Stimulus, Contrapositive Logic, Missing Link / Idea, Relative vs. Absolute
345	STIMULUS: Principle: It is healthy for children to engage in an activity that promotes their intellectual development only if engaging in that activity does not detract from their social development. Application: Although Megan’s frequent reading stimulates her intellectually, it reduces the amount of time she spends interacting with other people. Therefore, it is not healthy for her to read as much as she does.
346	STEM: The application of the principle is most vulnerable to criticism on which one of the following grounds?
347	(A) [4% | Not a Misinterpretation] It misinterprets the principle as a universal claim intended to hold in all cases without exception, rather than as a mere generalization.
348	(B) [4% | Unrelated to Goal] It overlooks the possibility that the benefits of a given activity may sometimes be important enough to outweigh the adverse health effects.
349	(C) [11% | Not a Misinterpretation] It misinterprets the principle to be, at least in part, a claim about what is unhealthy, rather than solely a claim about what is healthy.
350	(D) [73% | Correct] It takes for granted that any decrease in the amount of time a child spends interacting with others detracts from that child’s social development.
351	(E) [8% | Wrong Flaw: not Necessary vs. Sufficient] It takes a necessary condition for an activity’s being healthy as a sufficient condition for its being so.
352	--- BREAKDOWN
353	## Principle
354	If ... then ... engaging in an activity not healthy that promotes for children intellectual development to engage would detract from their social development
355	## Application
356	Since .... Therefore ... engaging in frequent not healthy reading (stimulates for Megan to Megan intellectually engage in this but) reduces the amount much reading of time Megan spends interacting with other people.
357	## Evaluate
358	The flow of these Application questions is that we're given a conditional rule. Then we're given an attempted Application of it. Someone tries to use this principle to get from their Evidence to their Conclusion. The Conclusion of the Application always (or almost always) matches the right side of the principle. But the Evidence fails to clearly establish all the ideas the left side (trigger) of the principle needs to know. The conditional indicator words "only if" mean that the idea that follows belongs on the right side. So when we see "only if engaging does not detract", we'd put that idea on the right side of the arrow. → doesn't detract from social development The other half of the sentence, "healthy to engage", would thus go on the left. healthy → doesn't detract from social development Since the conclusion of the Application is saying "not healthy", we want the contrapositive version of this rule, so that our right side will match our Conclusion: does detract from social development ⇢ not healthy Okay, so we're concluding "not healthy to read this much". So did the evidence establish that "reading this much is detracting from social development?" No, the closest it came to talking like that was when it said, "it reduces the amount of time she spends interacting with other people". Apparently the author is assuming that "if you reduce the amount of time you spend interacting with other people, then you're detracting from social development". Do we have to believe that? Not necessarily. We don't know if every extra hour you spend interacting with people contributes meaningfully to your social development.
359	## Goal
360	Look for something discussing the gap between "reduces the amount of time spent interacting with other people" and "detracts from her social development". (technically, we also need to say that Megan is a child, for the principle to apply to her)
361	--- CHOICE NOTES
362	(A) Principles, if they're worded conditionally like this one, are universal claims intended to hold in all cases without exception.
363	A generalization would be something like, "Activities that detract from your social development tend to be unhealthy". That sort of principle isn't intended to hold in all cases. But a claim structured with only if does not tolerate exceptions.
364	(B) We're supposed to be commenting on how the Application failed to trigger the Principle (because there's a difference between "less time spent interacting with people" and "detracting from social development").
365	This answer feels like an objection to the conclusion. "Hey, what do you mean it's not healthy! Just because it detracts from her social development, it still has enough blessings that it's worth it!"
366	This would be a critique of the conclusion, or a critique of the principle itself, but we're supposed to be critiquing the application, i.e. how the evidence failed to trigger the principle.
367	(C) This principle is absolutely about what is unhealthy. It says:
368	"if an activity detracts from social development, then it is unhealthy for children to engage in it"
369	(D) This calls out the missing link between the left side (trigger) of the Principle and the evidence of the Application.
370	evidence
371	reading so much reduces time spent interacting with others
372	trigger
373	the activity detracts from the child's social development
374	As this answer says, the author is assuming that "if it reduces time spent interacting with others, then it detracts from social development".
375	(E) This is describing the famous flaw in which an author provides a conditional statement in the premise and then applies it illegally in a backwards or opposite fashion to get to her conclusion.
376	This author didn't try to go backwards or inverted with a conditional statement. She's using it in its proper order. She just hasn't established the trigger of "detracting from social development".
377

439	=================== PT139-S4-Q6 | diff 2 | key D | score 18.7 | features: Principles in Stimulus
440	STIMULUS: A first-term board member should not be on the finance committee unless he or she is an accountant or his or her membership on the committee is supported by all the members of the board.
441	STEM: Which one of the following arguments most closely conforms to the principle stated above?
442	(A) [4% | Missing #3] Simkins is a first-term board member and not an accountant; thus, Simkins should not be on the finance committee.
443	(B) [2% | Bad Conclusion] Timmons is a third-term board member but not an accountant; thus, if all other board members think that Timmons should be on the finance committee, then Timmons should be on that committee.
444	(C) [10% | Unclear Application] Ruiz is on the finance committee but is not an accountant; thus, Ruiz's membership must have been supported by all the members of the board.
445	(D) [81% | Correct] Klein is a first-term board member who is not an accountant; thus, Klein should not be allowed on the finance committee if any board member opposes Klein's appointment to that committee.
446	(E) [3% | Missing #1] Mabry is a board member who is not an accountant; thus, because Mabry's membership on the finance committee is opposed by most board members, Mabry should not be allowed on that committee.
447	--- BREAKDOWN
448	## Principle
449	Most principles are conditional, and this one has an unless. When we see that trigger word, how do we turn it into a conditional? f.e. "A shouldn't happen, unless B happens" One method people use for 'unless' is to replace it with 'if-not'. "A shouldn't happen, if B not happens", which looks like ~B → ~A Another method people use is to translate the sentence into 'X requires Y' wording. When we hear "A shouldn't happen unless B happens", we can re-write that as "A requires B" which looks like A → B. Naturally, we'll get the same conditional either way. These two methods just yielded a contrapositive version of the other: ~B → ~A A → B The conditional principle here is more complex: X shouldn't happen unless Y or Z. If we do the 'if-not' method, then we're saying X shouldn't happen if not (Y or Z) If we're saying "it's not the case that Y or Z is true", that's akin to saying "both Y and Z are false". Negating (Y or Z) means we get ~Y and ~Z. Whenever we're negating an idea, Or turns to And and vice versa. ~Y and ~Z → X If we used the "turn it into A requires B" method, then "X shouldn't happen unless Y or Z is true" would become "X requires Y or Z", which looks like X → Y or Z So the two ways to write this rule would be this: X → Y or Z ~Y and ~Z → X Do we know / care which way we write it? Yes! We want to get the "judgy" (the normative / evaluative) term on the right. What do we mean by judgy? - good / bad - justified / unjustified - should / shouldn't - permissible / impermissible Stuff like that. In this principle the words "should not be" are the judgment words. So the right side looks like this. should not be → on finance committee We need to put all the qualifying criteria on the left side: 1st-term board member and should not be not an accountant → on finance and committee membership not supported by all
450	## Goal
451	Okay, the hard part is over. When we read answer choices, we know that the only legal conclusion is "should not be on committee". If any answers are saying "should be", they're automatically wrong.
452	--- CHOICE NOTES
453	(A) To trigger this principle and thus derive that someone shouldn't be on the finance committee, we need to establish three things
454	1. First term board member
455	2. Not an accountant
456	3. Membership not supported by all of board
457	This answer never tells us that Simkins membership isn't supported by all the members of the board. If they all support her, she could still have a chance of being on the finance committee.
458	(B) We don't need to read this one, if we scan for the conclusion and see that it's trying to prove someone "should be" on the committee.
459	The rule only has the power to prove "should not be", because the conclusion has to match the Right Side of the arrow.
460	(C) We're told that Ruiz is on the finance committee, but not that he should be on the finance committee. So it's not clear we can even apply this rule to his situation.
461	But if we pretended that part was okay, the answer still has a problem.

821	STIMULUS: Copernicus’s astronomical system is superior to Ptolemy’s and was so at the time it was proposed, even though at that time all observational evidence was equally consistent with both theories. Ptolemy believed that the stars revolved around the earth at great speeds.

823	STEM: The argument most closely conforms to which one of the following principles?
824	(A) [3% | Too Strong: sole] Simplicity should be the sole deciding factor in choosing among competing scientific theories.
825	(B) [13% | Bad Evidence Match] If one theory is likely to be true, and another competing theory is likely to be false, then the one likely to be true is the superior of the two.
826	(C) [20% | Weak Evidence/Conclusion Match] If all observational evidence is consistent with two competing theories, the one that is more intuitively true is the more practical theory to adopt.
827	(D) [55% | Correct] Other things being equal, the more complex of two competing theories is the inferior theory.
828	(E) [8% | Weaker Conclusion Match] Other things being equal, the simpler of two competing theories is the more scientifically important theory.

831	Copernicus's system is superior to Ptolemy's, even when it was proposed.
832	## Evidence
833	Ptolemy believed that the stars revolved around the earth at great speeds.
834	  Copernicus correctly thought that a simpler theory is that the earth rotates on its axis.
835	## Evaluate
836	This paragraph doesn't read too much as an *argument*, but the first sentence *is* an opinion ("superior"). Our usual goal when we're matching a Principle to an argument is to make the conclusion be the outcome of the principle, and a consideration from the evidence to be the trigger of the principle.
837	  If the conclusion is saying "one system is superior to another", then we want a principle that helps us judge the *superiority* of a theory.
838	  Why does our author think C's theory was better than P's theory?
839	  P posited something that C found unlikely. And C ** thought of a simpler theory.
840	  We obviously know that C's theory is borne out as the correct one, but why did the author find it superior *at the time it was proposed*, even though the available evidence was equally consistent with both theories?
841	  The superiority must have been its simplicity. That's the famous principle known as Ockham's Razor (the literal phrasing is something like "avoid superfluous entities", but the usage is that "all other things being equal, the simpler explanation is the better one").
842	## Goal
843	Look for something like, "if all the evidence fits both equally, the simpler theory is the superior one".

845	(A) The author thinks simplicity is the deciding factor here, but only because all observational evidence was equally consistent. The compatibility between theory and observational evidence is also something the author thinks is a deciding factor.

858	(D) Hey, it's Ockham's Razor! This has the 1/2 Evidence, 1/2 Conclusion language match we seek in Principle answers.

942	STIMULUS: Editorial: In order to encourage personal responsibility in adults, society should not restrict the performance of any of the actions of adults or interfere with the likely results except to prevent negative effects on others.
943	STEM: Which one of the following expresses a view that is inconsistent with the principle stated in the editorial?
944	(A) [8% | Consistent] We should not prevent the students from wasting the classroom time set aside for homework. But this does not mean that they may spend the time any way they wish. Activities disruptive to others should not be tolerated.
945	(B) [11% | Consistent] The scientist who invented this technology is not the only one who should be allowed to profit from it. After all, there is no evidence that allowing others to profit from this technology will reduce the scientist's own profits.
946	(C) [22% | Consistent] Even though public smoking may lead to indirect harm to others, it should not be banned. There are several other ways to eliminate this harm that do not restrict the conduct of smokers and hence are preferable to a complete ban on public smoking.
947	(D) [8% | Consistent] Highway speed limits are a justified restriction of freedom. For drivers who speed do not risk only their own lives; such drivers often injure or kill other people. Moreover, speed limits have been shown to significantly reduce highway accident and fatality rates.
948	(E) [50% | Correct] It is not enough that consumable products containing harmful substances have warning labels. Many adults simply ignore such warnings and continue to consume these substances in spite of the harm it may cause them. This is why consuming such substances should be illegal.
949	--- BREAKDOWN
950	## Principle
951	wouldn't be preventing society shouldn't negative effects on others → restrict actions of adults
952	## Evaluate / Goal
953	Since we're looking to be inconsistent with a principle, we're basically trying to contradict it. Whenever we're contradicting a conditional / refuting a conditional, we're looking for a case where the Trigger happens but the Outcome doesn't happen. In the case of this rule, we want a situation where "society isn't preventing negative effects on others (a victimless crime), and they ARE restricting the actions of adults.
954	--- CHOICE NOTES
955	(A) In this situation, an action is being restricted. Are told that the action wouldn't have negative effects on others?
956	The opposite. The action being forbidden is labeled as "disruptive to others", so it does have negative effects on others, hence this principle doesn't apply to the situation (we can't violate a principle that isn't applicable).
957	(B) This prevents the action of "restricting profit to only the inventor". Are we told that this action has no negative effects on others? No, we're not. In fact, it's pretty common sensical that "keeping profits from others" has a negative effect on them (they don't get any money).
958	This argument is using a Negated principle from the one we got. It is thinking:
959	if an action (sharing profits) wouldn't harm others, then we should allow it
960	Adhering to a negated-version of a Principle is not the same as contradicting a Principle.
961	Given the principle: "If A, then B"
962	Adhering to negated-version: X is not A. So, X is not B."
963	Contradicting the principle: "X is A, but X is not B."
964	(C) Since smoking does have negative effects (indirect harm) on others, this isn't what we're looking for.
965	(D) We're talking about speed limits restricting the action of speeding, and we're told that speeding can have negative effects on others (often injure or kill).
966	(E) We're talking about banning the act of consuming a product that contains harmful substances (i.e. beer, cigarettes, cannabis). Does consuming this product have a negative effect on others? There's nothing in the text that says it does. The text emphasizes that harm would come to he/she who consumes the substance.
967	We might be tempted to bring in our outside knowledge of negative effects caused by second hand smoking, alcoholism, mediocre stoner comedies, etc., but on the face of it, consuming a harmful substance only harms you. This text doesn't say otherwise.
968	Usually, on LSAT, we don't want to assume that "if it wasn't mentioned, it isn't applicable". There may very well be negative effects on others that come from consuming harmful substances. But the other answers made the negative effects on others explicit, whereas this one says nothing. Thus, it's the closest we can come to violating the principle from the available answers.

1454	STIMULUS: In a party game, one person leaves the room with the understanding that someone else will relate a recent dream to the remaining group. Surprisingly, the person usually constructs a dream narrative that is both coherent and ingenious.
1455	STEM: The example presented above most closely conforms to which one of the following propositions?
1456	(A) [82% | Correct] The presumption that something has order and coherence can lead one to imbue it with order and coherence.
1457	(B) [4% | Opposite] One is less apt to reach a false understanding of what someone says than to make no sense out of it at all.
1458	(C) [2% | Unsupported] Dreams are often just collections of images and ideas without coherent structures.
1459	(D) [7% | Too Strong: requires] Interpreting another person's dream requires that one understand the dream as a coherent narrative.
1460	(E) [4% | Doesn't Match] People often invent clever and coherent stories to explain their behavior to other people.
1461	--- BREAKDOWN
1462	## Situation
1463	Eddie leaves the room. He thinks that Marsha is going to tell everyone about a dream she recently had. When he's allowed to return to the room, he tries to figure out what her dream was all about by asking everyone in the room Yes / No questions. But Marsha never actually told them any dream. They just created some rule for when they'll say Yes / When they'll say no. (e.g. Every 3rd question, say "yes" / If your name starts with a vowel, say "yes") By piecing together the Yes and No answers that he gets, Eddie actually ends up thinking he has figured out the story, and the story he thinks he's figured out is coherent and ingenious.
1464	## Evaluate
1465	When we're asked to listen to a situation / story and then figure out which generalization / proposition it best illustrates or matches, we're hoping to extract from the stimulus some safely worded takeaway. Most of the wrong answers are too strongly worded. We use our Inference brain and look primarily for Causal or Comparison/Contrast language to figure out what sort of synthesis we could say. Here, there's a contrast (dramatic irony) between Eddie and the rest of the partygoers. They know there is no actual story, and are just giving random Yes/No answers. He thinks there is an actual story, and the one he is guessing is surprisingly coherent.
1466	## Goal
1467	Look for an answer that Straddles the Pivot, that synthesizes what's going on with the partygoers with the surprising effect it's having on Eddie.
1468	--- CHOICE NOTES
1469	(A) This has lovable safe language "X can lead to Y". It ends up being the most supportable answer, but it's kind of obnoxious.

Eddie thinks that the partygoers were told a story with order and coherence".
1472	Because Eddie thought a real dream story was told, he interprets the random Yes/No answers given by the partygoers in a way that results in his version of the dream having surprising "coherence".
1473	(B) This would maybe be okay if the answer said "more apt".
1474	Eddie was receiving a nonsensical, random series of Yes/No answers. From that, he reached a false understanding of what Marsha's dream story was (there was no story, but he thinks there was).
1475	So rather than making no sense at all out of what the partygoers were saying, Eddie reached a false understanding.
1476	(C) We don't really have any evidence of what dreams are like, in the sense that Marsha didn't actually tell anyone about her dream.
1477	But in the sense that Eddie made a coherent story out of the Yes/No answers, Eddie's conception of the dreams definitely DID have coherence.
1478	(D) This is the typical kind of trap answer for this sort of question, where it more or less reinforces the story but with language that is far too amped up.
1479	Eddie's interpretation of what he thought the dream was involved coherence, but we can't say from this that interpreting a dream requires that one understand it as a narrative.
1480	(E) Eddie came the closest to "inventing" a clever and coherent story, but he wasn't trying to explain his behavior. He was trying to reconstruct Martha's dream.

1850	=================== PT159-S3-Q14 | diff 3 | key D | score 13.8 | features: Important Pivot, Principles in Answers, Proposition/Generalization
1851	STIMULUS: Generally, open discussion of a false and pernicious idea diminishes its appeal. Even in a flourishing democracy, however, certain misconceptions are so alluring that they tend to gain in popularity when they are debated.
1852	STEM: Which one of the following principles is most clearly illustrated by the statements above?
1853	(A) [2% | Unsupported Recommendation: governments should] Democratic governments should be allowed on occasion to suppress debate regarding certain misconceptions.
1854	(B) [14% | Unsupported Comparison: Sometimes it is better] Sometimes it is better to openly discuss an idea even though doing so will increase the popularity of a misconception.
1855	(C) [4% | Unsupported Relationship: pernicious ideas / misconceptions] Not all pernicious ideas are misconceptions.
1856	(D) [69% | Correct] Discussion and debate do not necessarily lead to a greater acceptance of the truth.
1857	(E) [12% | Unsupported Causal Relationship: false / appealing] One of the reasons some ideas are appealing is that they are false.
1858	--- BREAKDOWN
1859	## Task
1860	The uncommon stem makes this question hard to categorize. "Which of the following principles" is common phrasing for both Principle Strengthen and Principle Conform, but "most clearly illustrated by the statements above" is more typical of Proposition / Generalization questions, a subtype of Most Supported. The task for that subtype is to extract a proposition / generalization / principle from the reading, which often feels like articulating the "moral of the story." That will be the task for this question.
1861	## Given
1862	Generally, open discussion of bad and dangerous ideas makes them less appealing. However, even in democracies, certain bad ideas are so attractive that they get more appealing when they are discussed openly.
1863	## Evaluate
1864	Proposition / Generalization questions often revolve around an important pivot word that creates tension in the stimulus. Our job is to find an answer that does the best job articulating that tension. Our first claim is something that is generally true: open discussion lessens the appeal of wrong ideas. After the pivot (however), we get tension in the form of an exception: some bad ideas are so appealing they get more attractive when debated.
1865	## Goal
1866	Look for an answer that articulates that tension. Beware strong language, new ideas, and unsupported relationships. Lean towards the weakest, most provable answer.
1867	--- CHOICE NOTES
1868	(A) Whenever a Most Supported answer presents a recommendation, be skeptical! If the stimulus didn't say that specific party should act in that specific way, the answer is almost certainly wrong. Additionally, the stimulus expresses a tension surrouding open debate, but it doesn't suggest how we should respond to that tension. This answer is too speculative because it recommends a specific, unsupported response.
1869	(B) Whenever a Most Supported answer presents a comparison, be skeptical! If the stimulus didn't compare those two specific things on that one specific metric, the answer is probably wrong. Additionally, this answer has a similar problem as Answer Choice A: the stimulus expresses a tension surrouding open debate, but it doesn't suggest how we should respond to that tension. This answer is too speculative because it suggests one response is better than some unnamed alternative.
1870	(C) The weak degree of "not all" is attractive in Most Supported questions because it only requires one data point to prove. So, we ask ourselves: does this stimulus prove that there is at least one pernicious idea that is not a misconception? Nope. The only pernicious idea mentioned in the stimlus is "false and pernicious" which is by definition a misconception. While Answer Choice C may be true in real life, that doesn't mean it is well supported by this stimulus.
1871	(D) This articulates the tension expressed in the stimulus and sounds like the "moral of the story" we're being told.
1872	(E) The weak degree of "one of the reasons" and "some ideas" are both appealing in a Most Supported question because they only require one data point to prove. So, we ask ourselves: does this stimulus prove that there is at least one appealing idea that is appealing because it's false? Nope. We're told that certain false ideas are appealing, but we can't infer the Causal Relationship that they are appealing because of their falsity. Whenever a Most Supported answer presents a causal relationship, be skeptical and investigate it!
1873	
1874

1876	STIMULUS: Evan: I am a vegetarian because I believe it is immoral to inflict pain on animals to obtain food. Some vegetarians who share this moral reason nonetheless consume some seafood, on the grounds that it is not known whether certain sea creatures can experience pleasure or pain. But if it is truly wrong to inflict needless suffering, we should extend the benefit of the doubt to sea animals and refrain from eating seafood.
1877	STEM: Which one of the following most closely conforms to the principle illustrated by Evan’s criticism of vegetarians who eat seafood?
1878	(A) [78% | Correct] I do not know if I have repaid Farah the money she lent me for a movie ticket. She says that she does not remember whether or not I repaid her. In order to be sure that I have repaid her, I will give her the money now.
1879	(B) [8% | Bad Conclusion Match] It is uncertain whether all owners of the defective vehicles know that their vehicles are being recalled by the manufacturer. Thus, we should expect that some vehicles that have been recalled have not been returned.
1880	(C) [8% | Bad Premise/Conclusion Match] I am opposed to using incentives such as reduced taxes to attract businesses to our region. These incentives would attract businesses interested only in short-term profits. Such businesses would make our region’s economy less stable, because they have no long-term commitment to the community.
1881	(D) [4% | Bad Premise/Conclusion Match] Updating our computer security system could lead to new contracts. The present system has no problems, but we could benefit from emphasizing a state-of-the-art system in new proposals. If we do not get new customers, the new system could be financed through higher fees for current customers.
1882	(E) [2% | Bad Premise/Conclusion Match] Isabel Allende lived through the tragic events of her country’s recent history; no doubt her novels have been inspired by her memories of those events. Yet Allende’s characters are hopeful and full of joy, indicating that Allende’s own view of life has not been negatively marked by her experiences.
1883	--- BREAKDOWN
1884	## Evan's Criticism
1885	My pescatarian friends (vegetarians who otherwise eat fish) should not be eating fish.
1886	## Evan's Premise
1887	Even though we're not sure that sea creatures experience pleasure or pain, if we're trying to avoid inflicting pain, we should give them the benefit of the doubt (and act as though they DO feel pain, so we shouldn't murder them for our food).
1888	## Evaluate
1889	This is like a Parallel question, but it uses the language of "illustrated Principle". Some Parallel questions are testing formal logical structures (like "All X's are Y. All Y's are Z. Since this thing is not Z, it is not X"). Other Parallel questions are just testing slogan / principle type ideas: - if it was true before, it'll be true again - if the extra effort yield little in return, don't bother - if you behaved that way in this situation, you should behave the same way in a similar situation We need to think about what Principle Evan is implicitly using (but we can't name it using words like vegetarian / pain / seafood). He seems to think: if you aren't sure whether or not you're hurting something, err on the side of not hurting them by not risking it.
1890	## Goal
1891	Let's try to find an argument in which someone is saying, "You're usually worried about X. You know it's possible that X would happen with action Y. Even though you can't be sure, you should play it safe and not do action Y."
1892	--- CHOICE NOTES
1893	(A) This establishes a potential for wronging someone — we might be stiffing Farah on the money she lent us.
1894	It establishes that we're not sure whether we're wronging someone — Farah can't remember if she's been paid back.
1895	And it concludes that we should act on the safe side of making sure we're not wronging that someone — let's just pay her the money, so we can be sure we're not wronging her.
1896	We can match up those structural features with the original:
1897	Establishes a potential for wronging someone — we might be inflicting pain on sea creatures when we kill them for food
1898	Establishes that we're not sure whether we're wronging someone — it's not known whether certain sea creatures can experience pleasure / pain
1899	Concludes that we should act on the safe side of making sure we're not wronging that someone — let's just refrain from eating seafood.
1900	(B) This establishes a potential for wronging someone — some people might be be driving a defective vehicle unknowingly.
1901	It establishes that we're not sure whether we're wronging someone — "it is uncertain whether they know".
1902	But it fails to conclude that we should act on the safe side of making sure we're not wronging that someone — instead of saying "so we should just try contacting all the vehicle owners again to make sure they know", it says some mismatching conclusion that is more like "so some of the vehicles the car company recalled have not been returned by the vehicle owners".
1903	That would be like if the original argument said, "Thus, we should expect that some of sea creatures we eat do / do not feel pain".
1904	(C) It's hard to match any part of this up with the argument. The principle in this argument is something like, "Let's not do a certain action, because even if it would bring a short term gain, it will probably make us more shaky in the long run."
1905	(D) It's hard to match any part of this up with the argument. The principle in this argument is something like, "Let's do action X. Things are presently fine, but X might make them better, and if not, we can do things to offset the cost of X."
1906	(E) It's hard to match any part of this up with the argument. The principle in this argument is something like, "Since a certain person's art reflects traits that are a mismatch for the artists' experiences, the artist has not been tainted by her experiences."
