1910	STIMULUS: University president: When a faculty member’s falsification of research was uncovered, the media treated it as evidence of the university’s low standards, even though in truth it was a mere case of dishonesty. But since vigilance with respect to academic standards is always necessary, it’s good that standards have become a topic of discussion.
1911	STEM: Which one of the following conforms most closely to the principle illustrated above?
1912	(A) [2% | Bad Outcome Match] The latest government scandal was caused primarily by a lack of oversight, which in turn led to corruption. Since no amount of oversight can eliminate all corruption, it is important that the problems with oversight are not the only topic of discussion.
1913	(B) [91% | Correct] The latest government scandal has been attributed to lack of oversight, although the true cause of the scandal was simple corruption. Nonetheless, this discussion of oversight is welcome, because oversight is important in its own right.
1914	(C) [4% | Bad Outcome Match] The latest government scandal has been attributed to both lack of oversight and corruption. As a result, these important concerns are now being discussed. So, despite the harm that it caused, it is good that the scandal occurred.
1915	(D) [1% | Bad Outcome Match] The latest government scandal has been analyzed as a case of simple corruption, although corruption had little to do with it. Because the true cause of the scandal was lack of oversight, attributing the cause of the scandal to simple corruption is harmful.
1916	(E) [2% | Bad Trigger Match] The latest government scandal has been analyzed as a case of simple corruption, with no mention of the role played by lack of oversight. Nonetheless, the focus on corruption is welcome, because corruption played the largest role in the scandal.
1917	--- BREAKDOWN
1918	## Opposing Idea
1919	When a faculty member's falsification of research was uncovered, the media treated it as evidence of the university's low standards, even though in truth it was a mere case of dishonesty.
1920	## Evidence (since)
1921	Vigilance with respect to academic standards is always necessary
1922	## Conclusion
1923	It's good that standards have become a topic of discussion.
1924	## Evaluate
1925	When evaluating arguments in LR, we can often pretty much ignore any Opposing Idea that's presented. With question types like Assumption questions, Flaw, Strengthen, and Weaken, once we've identified the evidence and the conclusion we usually focus our attention there. In this question, we need to understand the principle that's illustrated by the argument. The correct answer choice will illustrate the same principle. In this case it helps to keep a more big-picture view, and notice exactly how the evidence and conclusion relate to the opposing idea. Here's how we might describe the argument as a whole:
1926	  "People blamed a situation on X, even though that wasn't the cause. But X is important, so it's good that X is being discussed."
1927	  If we think about the principle being illustrated, we might describe it this way:
1928	  "If something is important then it's good to talk about it, even if if the discussion came up for the wrong reasons."
1929	  A core feature of this principle is the conclusion that it's good for this discussion to happen.
1930	## Goal
1931	We'll look for an answer which matches this same overall pattern. Beware of answers that describe a different type of background scenario / opposing idea, or ones which draw a very different type of conclusion.
1932	--- CHOICE NOTES
1933	(A) This answer concludes that X should not be the only topic of discussion. The original states that it's good to talk about X, and doesn't address whether or not it should be the only topic of discussion.
1934	(B) We could abstractly describe this answer choice like this:
1935	People blamed a situation on X, but the true cause was Y.
1936	But X is important, so it's good that X is being discussed.

1947	=================== PT118-S3-Q18 | diff 3 | key E | score 12.7 | features: Implicit Principles
1948	STIMULUS: The tendency toward overspecialization in the study of artifacts is unfortunate. Scholars can enhance their understanding of a certain artistic period by studying art from earlier periods that had a significant influence on it. For instance, because of its influence on Spanish artisans, a proper understanding of Arabic porcelain is indispensable for a proper understanding of Spanish porcelain.
1949	STEM: Of the following, which one most closely conforms to the principle that the passage as a whole illustrates?
1950	(A) [2% | Bad Evidence Match (before vs. after)] To understand completely the major trends in research on aging, one must understand the influences these trends exert on society’s view of aging.
1951	(B) [13% | Weaker Evidence Match (similar vs. causal)] To understand fully the historical events of this century, a historian must have an understanding of similar events in earlier centuries.
1952	(C) [8% | Weaker Evidence Match (cousins vs. parent)] To appreciate fully the French language, one must understand the other languages that share its linguistic ancestry.
1953	(D) [1% | Bad Evidence Match] To understand properly any academic discipline, one must have at least a superficial acquaintance with the practices of the wider academic community.
1954	(E) [76% | Correct] To understand completely Aristotle’s philosophy, one must be well acquainted with the philosophy of his intellectual mentor, Plato.
1955	--- BREAKDOWN
1956	## Conclusion
1957	It's unfortunate how scholars studying artifacts tend to overspecialize nowadays (it'd be better if they had a broader range of expertise).
1958	## Evidence
1959	Their understanding of whatever art period they're studying could be enhanced by studying earlier art that influenced it.
1960	## Evaluate
1961	When we're trying to extract an implicit principle, we're sort of doing the upfront work of Parallel reasoning, except we wouldn't usually be diagramming anything, just trying to think of the general essence without referencing the specific topic.
1962	
1963	The passage is saying that focusing too much on one thing can lead to a deficient understanding of it. You could potentially understand that thing better by also focusing on some of its influential precursors.
1964	## Goal
1965	I'd probably be looking for something like,
1966	"You could better understand X if you also developed an appreciation for earlier stuff that influenced X."
1967	--- CHOICE NOTES
1968	(A) We wanted something like, "to understand X, you need to also understand the **earlier** stuff that **influenced** X."
1969	
1970	This is saying "to understand X, you need to understand the **repercussions** of X, the stuff that X causes."
1971	
1972	This is focusing on what comes AFTER the thing we're studying, on how the thing we're studying affects other stuff.
1973	
1974	The original was focusing on what came BEFORE the thing we're studying, on how that earlier stuff affects what we're studying.
1975	(B) We wanted something like, "to understand X, you need to also understand the **earlier** stuff that **influenced** X."
1976	
1977	This is saying "to understand X, you need to also understand the earlier stuff that is **similar** to X."
1978	
1979	That's probably valid advice in and of itself, but not as good a match for the original, which specifically talking about earlier things that SHAPED the thing we're studying, not just earlier things that RESEMBLE the thing we're studying.
1980	(C) We wanted something like, "to understand X, you need to also understand X's backstory."
1981	
1982	This is saying "to understand X, you need to also understand other things that share X's backstory."
1983	
1984	The answer would be a better match if it were saying, "To fully appreciate French, you need to understand its linguistic ancestry", because that would talking about the precursors that influenced French.
1985	
1986	This isn't asking us to understand the previous generations (the parents); it's asking us to understand the current relatives (the cousins), the other languages that have a similar backstory to French.
1987	(D) We wanted something like, "to understand X, you need to also understand the **earlier** stuff that **influenced** X."
1988	
1989	This is saying "to understand X, you need to also understand other stuff currently going on outside of X."
1990	
1991	It doesn't have anything to do with the causal precursors of X.
1992	(E) We wanted something like, "to understand X, you need to also understand the **earlier** stuff that **influenced** X."
1993

1999	=================== PT130-S1-Q21 | diff 3 | key C | score 12.1 | features: Plans / Recommendations, Principles in Stimulus, Rebuttals
2000	STIMULUS: Columnist: Although there is and should be complete freedom of thought and expression, that does not mean that there is nothing wrong with exploiting depraved popular tastes for the sake of financial gain.
2001	STEM: Which one of the following judgments conforms most closely to the principle cited by the columnist?
2002	(A) [2% | Half Scope] The government should grant artists the right to create whatever works of art they want to create so long as no one considers those works to be depraved.
2003	(B) [7% | Half Scope] People who produce depraved movies have the freedom to do so, but that means that they also have the freedom to refrain from doing so.
2004	(C) [77% | Correct] There should be no laws restricting what books are published, but publishing books that pander to people with depraved tastes is not thereby morally acceptable.
2005	(D) [12% | Opposite] The public has the freedom to purchase whatever recordings are produced, but that does not mean that the government may not limit the production of recordings deemed to be depraved.
2006	(E) [2% | Opposite] One who advocates complete freedom of speech should not criticize others for saying things that he or she believes to exhibit depraved tastes.
2007	--- BREAKDOWN
2008	## Principle
2009	There should be complete freedom of thought and expression, as there is. But it's still wrong to exploit depraved popular tastes for the sake of financial gain.
2010	## Evaluation
2011	Usually, principles are conditional statements. This one, though, seems more like balancing ideas that are somewhat in tension. Yes, we should allow free expression, but we can still condemn when people try to make money off other people's depraved tastes. I think this is saying something like, "A lot of people like violent movies, but this is an unhealthy depraved taste. People who want to make a lot of money off making violent movies should be free to do so, but we can still judge them negatively for doing so."
2012	## Goal
2013	Look for a situation where we're scorning someone for doing X, but not forbidding them to do X, where X is meant to make money off of people's unhealthy tastes.
2014	--- CHOICE NOTES
2015	(A) This has the "let them do X" part, but not the part about "scorn them for making money off people's depraved tastes".
2016	(B) This has the "let them do X" part, but not the part about "scorn them for making money off people's depraved tastes". (For a second there, I thought I had accidentally predicted the topic of the correct answer!)
2017	(C) This combines both things we wanted: you're free to do it, but we can still scold you for doing it.
2018	(D) If the government can limit was sort of recordings are produced, then we haven't achieved the "complete freedom of expression" the principle discussed. In addition, this answer doesn't have the 2nd ingredient, which says that "we can still say that what you did was wrong, even though you're free to do it".
2019	(E) This starts off bad because it's talking about people who advocate complete freedom of speech, when the principle is not about advocates of freedom, it's about there actually being freedom. The 2nd ingredient is drifting towards the opposite, saying that we should not criticize depraved things. The principle says we can still criticize people for trying to make money off depraved things.
2020

They also explain the payoff: with two rules, the OR rule is usually the one tested (PT115-S2-Q11, PT139-S4-Q6, PT152-S2-Q21).\n- **When the principle is implicit, they abstract it to a topic-free slogan before reading the choices.** Then they name the exact relationship each trap swaps: before vs after, cause vs similarity, parent vs cousin, \"even if\" vs \"as long as\" (PT118-S3-Q18, PT121-S1-Q4, PT144-S2-Q26, PT153-S3-Q25).\n- **They rewrite the popular trap into the version that would have been right.** This shows the student exactly which element broke (PT153-S3-Q25 A, golden PT143-S1-Q6 style; PT103-S3-Q25 C).\n- **They say what the stem wording changes.** \"Conforms\" or \"underlies\" is not \"if valid, justify\", so an answer that would prove the conclusion can still be wrong (PT101-S2-Q22 A). \"Violate\" and \"conflict\" mean the trigger applies and the outcome is defied (PT120-S1-Q7, PT142-S4-Q21). \"Cannot always be followed\" asks for a case where compliance is impossible (PT120-S1-Q24).\n- **For analogies, they test each principle against both halves** (PT148-S4-Q6).\n- **They use a worked example where a meaning match is the hard step.** Examples: the wolf timeline (PT135-S4-Q23), $10M plus $2M (PT144-S2-Q26), the water gun (PT120-S1-Q24).\n- **They catch quantifier and scope slips inside otherwise good answers.** Examples: one person vs people generally, many vs most, \"those who feel most strongly\" vs \"everyone\" (PT126-S1-Q21); \"never\" vs \"balance\" (PT102-S2-Q24).\n\n### What the weaker candidates got wrong\n\n- **Wrong type for the family.** Several shortlisted slots are really Flaw (PT134-S2-Q23), Most Supported (PT159-S3-Q14, PT136-S2-Q9), principle-Weaken (PT149-S4-Q17) or principle-Paradox (PT142-S4-Q12). I picked 20 models for each type after reading all 80 candidates in full and checking every choice note against the printed choice text. Both shortlists had enough strong candidates, so I didn't look beyond them in `scores.json`. I edited no other files and wrote nothing to any database.

**Principle-Strengthen (20 picks)**
- **Spread:** 4 at difficulty 1, 5 at 2, 5 at 3, 2 at 4 and 4 at 5.
- **Sub-patterns:** principles in the answers, a rule given in a premise or in the stimulus, weighing tradeoffs, two speakers, comparative and consistency conclusions, and an answer aimed at the intermediate conclusion.
- **Top-rated (12/12):** PT146-S1-Q1 and PT144-S3-Q23.
- **Already golden:** PT155-S4-Q9 is the only one.
- **What the best ones share:** they predict a rule that ends in the conclusion's judgment, and they rule out answers by outcome first, then by whether the trigger applies ("flexible on the trigger, stubborn on the outcome"). They translate "only if", "unless" and "if and only if" and spot the correct answer hidden in contrapositive form. They switch to weighing tradeoffs when the argument has pros and cons, and they give the most popular wrong answer the longest note.

**Principle-Conform (20 picks)**
- **Spread:** 6 at difficulty 2, 3 at 3, 5 at 4 and 6 at 5 (none at 1).
- **Sub-patterns:** principle in the stimulus, including two-rule and checklist cases; implicit or illustrated principles; analogies; and unusual stems (violates, conflicts with, cannot always be followed). One pick is a principle-assumption question, since that stem sits in this family's bucket.
- **Already golden:** none of the picks. The two existing goldens (PT135-S1-Q6, PT151-S2-Q4) are noted but not re-picked.
- **What the best ones share:** when the principle is in the stimulus, they sort answers by what the rule can conclude, then tick off the trigger as a checklist. They negate AND/OR correctly. For implicit principles, they boil the principle down to a slogan with no topic words, then name exactly what each trap swaps (before vs after, cause vs similarity, "even if" vs "as long as"). They also say what "conforms", "violates" and "if valid" each change about the task.

**Problems to fix before reuse**
- **Disqualify PT101-S2-Q25:** its notes for C and D describe choices that aren't on the page.
- **Wrong letter in PT119-S3-Q8:** the correct-answer note ends by naming (E).
